</div>
</div></section></section><section aria-label="Article content"><section class="body main-article-body"><section class="abstract" id="abstract1"><h2>Abstract</h2>
<p>The COVID-19 pandemic has caused over 7 million deaths globally in the past four years. <em>Siparuna</em> spp. (Siparunaceae), which is used in Brazilian folk medicine, is considered a genus with potential antiviral alternatives. This study explored the correlation between phytochemicals in <em>Siparuna</em> leaf extracts (<em>S. ficoides</em>, <em>S. decipiens</em>, <em>S. glycycarpa</em>, <em>S. reginae</em>, and <em>S. cymosa</em>) and their potential against various SARS-CoV-2 targets. In vitro assays examined interactions between the spike protein and the ACE2 receptor, protease activity, and viral replication inhibition in Calu-3 cell models. UHPLC-MS/MS analysis, processed with MZmine and evaluated chemometrically, revealed isoquinoline alkaloids with bulbocapnine, showing promising therapeutic potential. Predictions regarding absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion, and toxicity were conducted, along with molecular docking and dynamics simulations, to evaluate protein−ligand interaction stability. The results confirmed the antiviral activity of the <em>Siparuna</em> genus against SARS-CoV-2 targets, with 92% of the extracts maintaining over 70% cellular viability at 200 μg·mL<sup>−1</sup> and 80% achieving more than 50% viral activity suppression at 50 μg·mL<sup>−1</sup>. These findings highlight the potential of isoquinoline alkaloids as novel anti-coronavirus agents and support the need for further exploration, isolation, and testing of <em>Siparuna</em> compounds in the fight against COVID-19.</p>
<section id="kwd-group1" class="kwd-group"><p><strong>Keywords:</strong> Brazilian folk medicine, <em>Siparuna</em> spp., SARS-CoV-2, UHPLC-MS/MS, chemometric analyses, molecular docking, dynamics simulations, ADMET predictions, bulbocapnine</p></section></section><section id="sec1-ijms-26-00633"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">1. Introduction</h2>
<p>Members of the Coronaviridae family include seasonal and highly pathogenic viruses [<a href="#B1-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-ijms-26-00633">1</a>]. The severe acute respiratory syndrome coronavirus 2 (SARS-CoV-2) caused a significant global impact, with over 775 million cases and 7 million deaths resulting from coronavirus disease 2019 (COVID-19) until 2024 [<a href="#B2-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B2-ijms-26-00633">2</a>,<a href="#B3-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-ijms-26-00633">3</a>]. The COVID-19 pandemic significantly impacted Brazil, marked by the co-circulation of multiple variants resulting from multiple independent introduction events over time. According to the WHO, Brazil reported more than 37 million confirmed cases and more than 702 thousand deaths by April 2024, making it one of the countries most affected by this disease [<a href="#B4-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B4-ijms-26-00633">4</a>,<a href="#B5-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B5-ijms-26-00633">5</a>].</p>
<p>SARS-CoV-2 is a positive-sense single-stranded RNA virus with a genome size of approximately 30 kb. Its genome contains several key regions that encode both structural and non-structural proteins that are essential for viral particle assembly, replication, and successful infection [<a href="#B6-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B6-ijms-26-00633">6</a>,<a href="#B7-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-ijms-26-00633">7</a>]. Since 2020, significant progress has been made in the research of anti-SARS-CoV-2 compounds, with different viral proteins emerging as critical targets for combating COVID-19 [<a href="#B1-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-ijms-26-00633">1</a>,<a href="#B8-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-ijms-26-00633">8</a>,<a href="#B9-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-ijms-26-00633">9</a>,<a href="#B10-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-ijms-26-00633">10</a>,<a href="#B11-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-ijms-26-00633">11</a>,<a href="#B12-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-ijms-26-00633">12</a>,<a href="#B13-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B13-ijms-26-00633">13</a>,<a href="#B14-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-ijms-26-00633">14</a>], such as the Spike protein, focusing on its interaction with angiotensin-converting enzyme 2 (ACE2) receptors in host cells and chymotrypsin-like cysteine protease(3CL<sup>pro</sup>) and papain-like protease (PL<sup>pro</sup>), which are primarily responsible for proteolytic cleavage of polyproteins pp1a and pp1ab during the replication process [<a href="#B7-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B7-ijms-26-00633">7</a>,<a href="#B15-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B15-ijms-26-00633">15</a>,<a href="#B16-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B16-ijms-26-00633">16</a>]. Understanding the roles of these proteins during SARS-CoV-2 infection provides valuable insights for identifying and developing effective therapeutic strategies for the management of COVID-19.</p>
<p>Vaccination has significantly reduced the number of severe cases and fatalities associated with COVID-19. More than 10 billion vaccine doses have been administered globally since the beginning of the pandemic, shifting the severity of the disease from a potentially fatal to a mild/moderate hazard level [<a href="#B3-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B3-ijms-26-00633">3</a>,<a href="#B17-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B17-ijms-26-00633">17</a>,<a href="#B18-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B18-ijms-26-00633">18</a>]. Despite continued vaccination efforts, including guidelines recommending vaccine boosters, SARS-CoV-2 is still circulating, and the likelihood of new strain emergence highlights the need to develop new and effective therapeutic alternatives with broad activity against different variants of SARS-CoV-2 [<a href="#B19-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-ijms-26-00633">19</a>,<a href="#B20-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B20-ijms-26-00633">20</a>].</p>
<p>Recent studies showed that plant extracts, fractions, and isolated compounds can be used to target SARS-CoV-2, either by blocking CoVs binding to host-specific receptors, such as ACE2, or preventing RNA processing by inhibiting virus-encoded proteases, such as PL<sup>pro</sup> and 3CL<sup>pro</sup>, known as the main protease (M<sup>pro</sup>) [<a href="#B8-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B8-ijms-26-00633">8</a>,<a href="#B14-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-ijms-26-00633">14</a>,<a href="#B19-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-ijms-26-00633">19</a>,<a href="#B21-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-ijms-26-00633">21</a>,<a href="#B22-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B22-ijms-26-00633">22</a>], as well as inhibition of virus replication in cellular models (Human Lung Adenocarcinoma Cell Line Calu-3) assays [<a href="#B23-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-ijms-26-00633">23</a>,<a href="#B24-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-ijms-26-00633">24</a>].</p>
<p>Brazil has the greatest biodiversity in the world and a long tradition of using medicinal plants to treat major public health emergencies, making it a powerful place for discovering new antivirals [<a href="#B25-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B25-ijms-26-00633">25</a>,<a href="#B26-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B26-ijms-26-00633">26</a>,<a href="#B27-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B27-ijms-26-00633">27</a>]. Plants of the genus <em>Siparuna</em>, Siparunaceae family, are used in Brazilian folk medicine, especially in the Amazon region, for treating colds, fever, headaches, rheumatic pain, and gastrointestinal disorders, and have been studied for over 20 years by our research group [<a href="#B28-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-ijms-26-00633">28</a>,<a href="#B29-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-ijms-26-00633">29</a>,<a href="#B30-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-ijms-26-00633">30</a>,<a href="#B31-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-ijms-26-00633">31</a>].</p>
<p>The species <em>Siparuna apiosyce</em> (Mart.) A. DC (“limão bravo”) is mentioned in the first Brazilian Pharmacopeia due to its importance as an ingredient in syrup and cough drops [<a href="#B29-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B29-ijms-26-00633">29</a>]. Dichloromethane extracts from <em>Siparuna ficoides</em> Renner and Hausner (originally identified as <em>S. cristata</em> Poepp. and Endl. A.DC.) [<a href="#B32-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-ijms-26-00633">32</a>] contains the <em>O</em>-methylated flavonoids retusin and kumatakenin, which are able to inhibit the in vitro replication of SARS-CoV-2 with EC<sub>50</sub> as low as 0.6 and 0.3 μM, in Calu-3 cells, respectively. Furthermore, in silico studies utilizing the Pymol, UCSF Chimera, and AutoDock Vina software v.1.1.2. programs showed a potential interaction between these flavonoids and the viral proteases 3CL<sup>pro</sup> and PL<sup>pro</sup>, suggesting a possible mechanism of action [<a href="#B31-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-ijms-26-00633">31</a>,<a href="#B33-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-ijms-26-00633">33</a>]. Another in silico study, employing molecular docking and AutoDock Vina software v.1.1.2., analyzed the interaction of terpenes from the essential oil of <em>Siparuna guianensis</em> Aublet with SARS-CoV-2 proteins and ACE2, revealing considerable binding affinity of sesquiterpenes with the tested macromolecules, showing a promising herbal therapeutic adjuvant against SARS-CoV-2 infections [<a href="#B9-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B9-ijms-26-00633">9</a>].</p>
<p>Computational tools for drug design have emerged as a paramount approach for identifying bioactive compounds originating from natural products. The integration of various in vitro and in silico techniques has become a promising and advantageous strategy. These methods facilitate the precise and efficient analysis of drugs and bioactive compounds against specific protein targets [<a href="#B34-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B34-ijms-26-00633">34</a>,<a href="#B35-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B35-ijms-26-00633">35</a>]. In this manner, one can predict which metabolites are probable contributors to a specific biological activity, even prior to isolating the target compounds. This aids in the rational design of new therapeutic agents [<a href="#B36-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B36-ijms-26-00633">36</a>,<a href="#B37-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B37-ijms-26-00633">37</a>].</p>
<p>In this study, we combined biological and chemical screenings of <em>Siparuna</em> plant extracts with computational methods aiming to predict compounds with potential anti-SARS-CoV-2 activity from the species <em>Siparuna ficoides</em> Renner and Hausner; <em>Siparuna decipiens</em> (Tul.) A.DC., <em>Siparuna glycycarpa</em> (Ducke) Renner and Hausner, <em>Siparuna reginae</em> (Tul.) A.DC. and <em>Siparuna cymosa</em> Tolm (originally identified as <em>S. sarmentosa</em> Perkins) [<a href="#B32-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B32-ijms-26-00633">32</a>], thereby contributing to the discovery of natural product-based therapeutic options for COVID-19.</p></section><section id="sec2-ijms-26-00633"><h2 class="pmc_sec_title">2. Results and Discussion</h2>
<section id="sec2dot1-ijms-26-00633"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1. Siparuna spp. Extracts Against SARS-CoV-2 Targets</h3>
<p>Five Siparunas species, <em>S. ficoides</em> (SF), <em>S. decipiens</em> (SD), <em>S. glycycarpa</em> (SG), <em>S. reginae</em> (SR), and <em>Siparuna cymosa</em> (SC), were selected based on previous literature regarding their usefulness as sources of antiviral compounds [<a href="#B31-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-ijms-26-00633">31</a>,<a href="#B33-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-ijms-26-00633">33</a>]. Thus, in this work, we tested the ability of crude ethanol extracts (CEE) from their leaves, as well as the extracts obtained after liquid-liquid extraction (hexane-HEX, dichloromethane-DCM, ethyl acetate-EtOAc, and butanol-BuOH). All the samples were subjected to different approaches in order to identify any potential inhibitory activity against SARS-CoV-2: proteases (3CL<sup>pro</sup> and PL<sup>pro</sup>) and spike protein-ACE2 interaction.</p>
<section id="sec2dot1dot1-ijms-26-00633"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.1. Siparuna Extracts Inhibit Spike Protein-ACE2 Interaction</h4>
<p>The capacity of inhibiting RDB:ACE2 complex formation was evaluated using the immunoassay Lumit™ (Promega, Madison, WI, USA), which is a bioluminescence-based, no-wash, add-and-read kit designed for testing the potential of inhibitory samples [<a href="#B23-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B23-ijms-26-00633">23</a>]. Of the 25 assayed extracts, 16 were able to inhibit more than 50% of the Receptor Binding Domain (RBD) and ACE2 interaction (<a href="#ijms-26-00633-f001" class="usa-link">Figure 1</a>). Notably, the addition of SG (DCM, EtOAc, and BuOH), SR (CEE and EtOAc), and SC (CEE, EtOAc, and BuOH) extracts resulted in over 90% inhibition of RBD:ACE2 complex formation. Overall, the extracts obtained from the SG, SR, and SC species proved to be the most effective in blocking the entry of SARS-CoV-2 into cells. Only SD (CEE) did not interfere with the RBD:ACE2 interaction.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-26-00633-f001"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 1.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11765762_ijms-26-00633-g001.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e0e0/11765762/8459673a4810/ijms-26-00633-g001.jpg" loading="lazy" height="469" width="743" alt="Figure 1"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-26-00633-f001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Effect of <em>Siparuna</em> extracts on spike protein-ACE2 interaction. C: crude ethanolic extract; H: hexane; D: dichloromethane; E: ethyl acetate; B: butanol. Data are expressed as means of three individual experiments (<em>n</em> = 3). Dotted lines indicate 70% and 90% inhibition.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec2dot1dot2-ijms-26-00633"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.2. Siparuna Extracts Inhibit 3CL<sup>pro</sup> Activity</h4>
<p>SARS-CoV-2 3CL<sup>pro</sup> and PL<sup>pro</sup> proteolytic activities were assessed using the fluorescence resonance energy transfer (FRET) approach. This assay has already been applied to investigate potential enzyme activity inhibitors from natural sources [<a href="#B24-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B24-ijms-26-00633">24</a>]. Thirteen extracts successfully inhibited over 50% of 3CL<sup>pro</sup> activity (<a href="#ijms-26-00633-f002" class="usa-link">Figure 2</a>). Particularly, the inclusion of SD (EtOAc) extract led to over 90% inhibition of this protease in the conducted assay. Overall, SF, SG, and SC were the most promising, as all the tested extracts showed some potential for inhibiting protease activity. Only SD (CEE, HEX, and DCM) and SR (DCM and BuOH) did not interfere with 3CL<sup>pro</sup> activity.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-26-00633-f002"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 2.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11765762_ijms-26-00633-g002.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e0e0/11765762/478381e343d8/ijms-26-00633-g002.jpg" loading="lazy" height="459" width="733" alt="Figure 2"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-26-00633-f002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Effect of <em>Siparuna</em> extracts on 3CL<sup>pro</sup> activity. C: crude ethanolic extract; H: hexane; D: dichloromethane; E: ethyl acetate; B: butanol. Data are expressed as means of three individual experiments (<em>n</em> = 3). Dotted lines indicate 70% and 90% inhibition.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec2dot1dot3-ijms-26-00633"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">2.1.3. Siparuna Extracts Inhibit PL<sup>pro</sup> Activity</h4>
<p>Sixteen extracts successfully inhibited over 50% of PL<sup>pro</sup> viral activity (<a href="#ijms-26-00633-f003" class="usa-link">Figure 3</a>). Specifically, the inclusion of SG (CEE and HEX), SR (BuOH), and SC (CEE, DCM, EtOAc, and BuOH) extracts resulted in over 90% inhibition of this protease in the conducted assay. Consistent with what was observed previously for RBD:ACE2 inhibition, extracts from SG, SR, and SC demonstrated the highest inhibitory effect against PL<sup>pro</sup>. Only SF (HEX), SD (BuOH), SG (EtOAc and BuOH), and SR (EtOAc) did not interfere with PL<sup>pro</sup> activity.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-26-00633-f003"><h5 class="obj_head">Figure 3.</h5>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11765762_ijms-26-00633-g003.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e0e0/11765762/cf589a799b43/ijms-26-00633-g003.jpg" loading="lazy" height="473" width="766" alt="Figure 3"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-26-00633-f003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Effect of <em>Siparuna</em> extracts on PL<sup>pro</sup> activity. C: crude ethanolic extract; H: hexane; D: dichloromethane; E: ethyl acetate; B: butanol. Data are expressed as means of three individual experiments (<em>n</em> = 3). Dotted lines indicate 70% and 90% inhibition.</p></figcaption></figure><p>To establish a successful infection process, SARS-CoV-2 should bind to ACE2 and promote the fusion of the viral and cell membranes or induce particle endocytosis. This step is known as the virus entry process and occurs very early during infection. Natural compounds that inhibit the interaction between the S protein and ACE2 will directly aid in the management of COVID-19 by restricting the entry of SARS-CoV-2 into the host cell [<a href="#B38-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B38-ijms-26-00633">38</a>]. Similarly, inhibition of other targets, such as 3CL<sup>pro</sup> and PL<sup>pro,</sup> may suppress SARS-CoV-2 infection [<a href="#B39-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-ijms-26-00633">39</a>].</p>
<p>Based on our results, the species <em>S. cymosa</em> showed to be the most promising among the three evaluated activities, because all assayed extracts were able to block virus entry into the cell by inhibiting the RBD:ACE2 interaction. Additionally, they demonstrated the ability to interact with 3CL<sup>pro</sup> and PL<sup>pro</sup> proteases in varying proportions. The species <em>S. glycycarpa</em> also stood out for presenting one of the highest percentages of interaction against SARS-CoV-2 targets for the majority of the evaluated extracts, followed by <em>S. reginae</em>, <em>S. decipiens</em>, and <em>S. ficoides</em>, with emphasis on the extracts that showed the highest inhibition potential, namely EtOAc, BuOH, CEE, DCM, and HEX, respectively.</p>
<p>A previous study conducted by our research group showed that BuOH extracts from SG and SC were the most active, inhibiting 96.0 ± 1.3% and 89.5 ± 0.8% of influenza virus replication 24 h post-infection [<a href="#B31-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-ijms-26-00633">31</a>]. A bioassay-guided fractionation of the n-butanol extract of SG by centrifugal partition chromatography (CPC) reported annotated compounds in the CPC fractions belonging to the isoquinoline alkaloid class, flavonoid glycosides, and dihydrochalcones. Benzylisoquinoline and aporphine alkaloids were annotated in the most active fractions, whereas tetrahydroprotoberberines were the least active ones [<a href="#B40-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-ijms-26-00633">40</a>].</p>
<p>Recent studies on extracts from various plants have demonstrated their potential antiviral activities, suggesting their potential use as adjuvants in the treatment of SARS-CoV-2 infections. The ethanol extract of pomegranate peels (<em>Punica granatum</em> L.) used at different concentrations inhibited the interaction between Spike and ACE2 by up to 74%, and this effect was dose-dependent. This extract inhibited the activity of the viral 3CL<sup>pro</sup> up to 80% when used at 0.2 mg⋅mL<sup>−1</sup> [<a href="#B10-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B10-ijms-26-00633">10</a>]. Methanol extracts from turmeric (<em>Curcuma longa</em> L.) rhizomes, mustard (<em>Brassica nigra</em> L.) seeds, and wall rocket (<em>Diplotaxis erucoides</em> (L.) DC. subsp. <em>erucoides</em>) at 500 µg·mL<sup>−1</sup> displayed significant inhibition of the 3CL<sup>pro</sup> activity, resulting in inhibition protease activities of 100.0%, 90.6%, and 85.1%, respectively [<a href="#B11-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B11-ijms-26-00633">11</a>].</p>
<p>In vitro experiments for viral inhibition of SARS-CoV-2 revealed that whole-plant extracts (aqueous and alcoholic) of <em>Cissampelos pareira</em> L. could inhibit the virus by at least 60% SARS-CoV-2 replication in infected Vero cell cultures. The hydroalcoholic whole-plant extract showed an inhibition of 98%. The single-molecule constituents of <em>C. pareira</em> could also inhibit viral particles, with pareirarine (an isoquinoline alkaloid) showing the highest inhibition of 80%. Interestingly, the highest inhibition was shown by the whole-plant hydroalcoholic extract, which comprises various constituents. This suggests a synergistic effect of the constituents on viral inhibition [<a href="#B41-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B41-ijms-26-00633">41</a>].</p>
<p>The promising in vitro results obtained with plant extracts encourage the continuation of studies through their chemical analysis in the search for bioactive compounds that can be used as active principles in product formulations or as prototypes for the development of semi-synthetic derivatives, which may potentially act in the fight against emerging viral diseases, such as COVID-19 [<a href="#B42-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B42-ijms-26-00633">42</a>].</p></section></section><section id="sec2dot2-ijms-26-00633"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.2. Chemometric Analyses Using PLS Regression Model</h3>
<p>The analysis sought to predict the active compounds inhibiting the Spike:ACE2 interaction and the proteolytic activities of r3CL<sup>pro</sup> and rPL<sup>pro</sup>. Twenty-five extracts from the Siparunaceae family species were subjected to LC-MS/MS (ESI and APCI) analysis in positive and negative ionization modes. The chemical data were processed and concatenated using low-level data fusion. Chemometric analyses based on binary activity values in the three target models were performed. A Partial Least Squares (PLS) analysis model was constructed to correlate LC-MS data fusion with extract inhibitory activity.</p>
<p>The PLS regression employed the LC-MS dataset (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> ions and Retention Time—RT) of 75 samples as independent variables (X). The dependent variable (Y) reflected the inhibition activities against the three target models. This algorithm combines principal component analysis (PCA) and linear regression with the goal of identifying latent variables that optimize the correlation between predictors and biological activity.</p>
<p>To build the dataset of independent variables (X), a low-level data fusion strategy was employed. In this approach, spectral data from mass spectrometry analysis using ESI and APCI sources in positive and negative ionization modes were processed separately (normalization), concatenated, and formed an independent data matrix. The new dataset (low-level data matrix) was autoscaled. The low-level data matrix consisted of 75 rows (samples) and 460 columns (<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> ions and Retention Time), including ESI [+], 196 ions; ESI [−], 40 ions; APCI [+], 138 ions; and APCI [−], 86 ions. For the dataset of dependent variables (Y), three columns were constructed, one for each target model (Spike:ACE2; r3CL<sup>pro</sup>; rPL<sup>pro</sup>). Inhibition values were transformed into binary variables, with active samples (≥50% inhibition; 1) and non-active samples (&lt;50% inhibition; −1).</p>
<p>In this study, three models, with one target focused on individual targets (Spike:ACE2, r3CL<sup>pro</sup>, and rPL<sup>pro</sup>), were created. The X-loading weight plot highlights the ions that have the most positive impact in elucidating the desired activity, specifically those exclusively present and/or more abundant in the extracts exhibiting higher activity. For these analyses, the top 10 ions that contributed the most to explaining the investigated activity were arbitrarily selected.</p>
<p>Upon evaluating the top 10 ions with the highest likelihood of correlation with the activity in each target, it was noticeable that among these, seven ions reappeared as those with the greatest contribution to explaining the inhibition of the Spike:ACE2 interaction, the activity of r3CL<sup>pro</sup>, and rPL<sup>pro</sup>. The presence of these seven similar ions in the three single-target models provides insights into their potential roles in the conducted experimental assays and suggests the possible multitarget activity of these compounds.</p>
<p>In <a href="#ijms-26-00633-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>, <a href="#ijms-26-00633-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a> and <a href="#ijms-26-00633-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>, the green bars in the scores plot (A) represent active samples (≥50% inhibition), and red bars indicate inactive samples (&lt;50% inhibition). The height of the bars indicates the sample’s magnitude relative to the principal component, and the direction (upward or downward) indicates the position relative to the mean. In the loadings plot (B), bar height reflects each variable’s contribution to the principal component, with higher values indicating a stronger influence on the model. Seven highlighted ions were consistently among the top 10 most relevant across the three PLS models for the Spike (RBD):ACE2, 3CL<sup>pro</sup>, and PL<sup>pro</sup> targets, indicating their multitarget potential.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-26-00633-f004"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 4.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11765762_ijms-26-00633-g004.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e0e0/11765762/c52da71c3714/ijms-26-00633-g004.jpg" loading="lazy" height="421" width="723" alt="Figure 4"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-26-00633-f004/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Scores (<strong>A</strong>), X-loadings weight (<strong>B</strong>) for the PLS model based on the LC-MS data obtained in the low-level data matrix (concatenated ESI and APCI [+/−]) of the 75 samples of five species of the the Siparunaceae family, and the inhibition values of the extracts on the RBD:ACE2 interaction. C: crude ethanolic extract; H: hexane; D: dichloromethane; E: ethyl acetate; B: butanol.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-26-00633-f005"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 5.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11765762_ijms-26-00633-g005.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e0e0/11765762/dd0d6ff92329/ijms-26-00633-g005.jpg" loading="lazy" height="437" width="723" alt="Figure 5"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-26-00633-f005/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Scores (<strong>A</strong>), X-loadings weight (<strong>B</strong>) for the PLS model based on the LC-MS data obtained in the low-level data matrix (concatenated ESI and APCI [+/−]) of the 75 samples of five species of Siparunaceae family, and the inhibition values of the extracts on the r3CL<sup>pro</sup> activity. C: crude ethanolic extract; H: hexane; D: dichloromethane; E: ethyl acetate; B: butanol.</p></figcaption></figure><figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-26-00633-f006"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 6.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11765762_ijms-26-00633-g006.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e0e0/11765762/f038615f2e2f/ijms-26-00633-g006.jpg" loading="lazy" height="371" width="709" alt="Figure 6"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-26-00633-f006/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Scores (<strong>A</strong>), X-loadings weight (<strong>B</strong>) for the PLS model based on the LC-MS data obtained in the low-level data matrix (concatenated ESI and APCI [+/−]) of the 75 samples of five species of Siparunaceae family, and the inhibition values of the extracts on the rPL<sup>pro</sup> activity. C: crude ethanolic extract; H: hexane; D: dichloromethane; E: ethyl acetate; B: butanol.</p></figcaption></figure><p>In the investigation model of Spike:ACE2 interaction inhibitors (<a href="#ijms-26-00633-f004" class="usa-link">Figure 4</a>A,B), the explained variance was 99% (six factors), with R<sup>2</sup>, 0.99, and Q<sup>2</sup>, 0.87. The values of the Root Mean Square Error of Calibration (RMSEC) were 0.09, and those of the Root Mean Square Error of Cross-Validation (RMSECV) were 0.36. In this analysis, the ten ions with the greatest contribution to explaining the inhibition of the Spike:ACE2 interaction by the active extracts were [M + H]<sup>+</sup>
<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 268, 271, 286, 312, 314, 315, 317, 326, 328, and 330.</p>
<p>For the model r3CL<sup>pro</sup> activity inhibitors (<a href="#ijms-26-00633-f005" class="usa-link">Figure 5</a>A,B), 99% of the variance was explained by five factors, yielding an R<sup>2</sup> of 0.99 and Q<sup>2</sup> of 0.92. The RMSEC value was 0.06, and the RMSECV value was 0.21. In this analysis, of the 10 ions predicted as likely significantly related to explaining the inhibition of r3CL<sup>pro</sup> activity, seven ([M + H]<sup>+</sup>
<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 268, 286, 312, 314, 326, 328, and 330) had already been marked for the Spike:ACE2 model. Only the ions [M + H]<sup>+</sup>
<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 297, 609, and 625 are exclusive to this model.</p>
<p>Finally, in the model of rPL<sup>pro</sup> activity inhibitors (<a href="#ijms-26-00633-f006" class="usa-link">Figure 6</a>A,B), six factors explained 99% of the variance, resulting in an R<sup>2</sup> of 0.99 and a Q<sup>2</sup> of 0.88. The RMSEC value was 0.10, and the RMSECV value was 0.35. During this analysis, seven similar ions were previously predicted for the Spike:ACE2 and r3CL<sup>pro</sup> models were observed again. Meanwhile, the ions exclusive to the rPL<sup>pro</sup> model were [M + H]<sup>+</sup>
<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 359, 397, and 603.</p>
<p>The values of explained variance, R<sup>2,</sup> and Q<sup>2</sup>, as well as the lowest Root Mean Square Error (RMSE) for all obtained models, indicate a reliable fit, with the representativeness of the calibration data and the absence of overfitting. In all models, separation between the two distinct groups is evident (active and non-active samples). By selecting the 10 ions with the highest contribution, it is possible to ensure the investigation of ions with the highest likelihood of correlation with the potential activity in the three evaluated targets (multitarget), as well as streamline other computational assays, such as molecular network annotation, and studies of docking and molecular dynamics.</p>
<p>When evaluating the three models, it was possible to observe that the seven predicted ions (multitarget) had very close masses, within the range of [M + H]<sup>+</sup>
<em>m</em>/<em>z</em> 268–330. They also exhibited similar retention times (Rt: 9.0–11.6 min) and better ionization in positive mode, indicating possible nitrogenous and polar compounds. Therefore, it is conceivable that these ions belong to the same chemical class. Thus, these seven ions are of greater interest to be annotated and subjected to additional in silico assays, as they demonstrate the highest multitarget potential. Other ions predicted in the one-target models, such as <em>m</em>/<em>z</em> [M + H]<sup>+</sup> 271, 315, and 317 (for Spike), 297, 609, and 625 (for 3CL<sup>pro</sup>), and 397, 329, and 603 (for PL<sup>pro</sup>), were not considered as they were not significant in the multitarget model. Additionally, the fragmentation profiles appeared to be different from those of the predicted compounds in the multitarget model, suggesting the presence of molecules of the phenolic class.</p></section><section id="sec2dot3-ijms-26-00633"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.3. Annotated Compounds Using Custom Library</h3>
<p>After data processing, a few multitarget predicted ions emerged, which had already been reported in other works by our group as anti-influenza agents [<a href="#B31-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-ijms-26-00633">31</a>,<a href="#B40-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B40-ijms-26-00633">40</a>]. Therefore, their APCI/ESI-MS/MS fragmentation patterns were checked (<a href="#app1-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials Figures S1–S7</a>) and annotated (<a href="#ijms-26-00633-t001" class="usa-link">Table 1</a>).</p>

<td align="center" valign="middle" style="border-bottom:solid thin" rowspan="1" colspan="1">[<a href="#B31-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-ijms-26-00633">31</a>]</td>

</table></div>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/ijms-26-00633-t001/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>These compounds present the same fragmentation profile with a first loss of CH<sub>3</sub>NH<sub>2</sub> (−31 Da) for compounds <strong>2</strong>, <strong>3</strong>, and <strong>7</strong>, or NH<sub>3</sub> (−17 Da) for compounds <strong>1</strong>, <strong>4</strong>, <strong>5</strong>, and <strong>6</strong>. In addition, the losses of CH<sub>3</sub>OH (−32 Da) followed by CO (−28 Da) were observed when an OH and an OCH<sub>3</sub> group were vicinal in the aromatic ring (compounds <strong>1</strong>–<strong>7</strong>) and CH<sub>2</sub>O (−30 Da) followed by CO when there was a methylenedioxy group (compounds <strong>6</strong> and <strong>7</strong>) [<a href="#B45-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B45-ijms-26-00633">45</a>], characteristic of the isoquinoline alkaloids class (<a href="#ijms-26-00633-f007" class="usa-link">Figure 7</a>).</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-26-00633-f007"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 7.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11765762_ijms-26-00633-g007.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e0e0/11765762/829c9c861c83/ijms-26-00633-g007.jpg" loading="lazy" height="376" width="764" alt="Figure 7"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-26-00633-f007/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Structure of the chemical compounds identified in <em>Siparuna</em> spp. Coclaurine (<strong>1</strong>), reticuline (<strong>2</strong>), boldine (<strong>3</strong>), laurolitsine (<strong>4</strong>), asimilobine (<strong>5</strong>), actinodaphnine (<strong>6</strong>), and bulbocapnine (<strong>7</strong>).</p></figcaption></figure><p>In this way, it was possible to annotate seven isoquinoline alkaloids in the extracts that were most active against the different targets of SARS-CoV-2. Among them, five are aporphine alkaloids: actinodaphnine (<strong>6</strong>), boldine (<strong>3</strong>), laurolitsine (<strong>4</strong>), asimilobine (<strong>5</strong>), and bulbocapnine (<strong>7</strong>), and two are benzylisoquinolines alkaloids: coclaurine (<strong>1</strong>) and reticuline (<strong>2</strong>).</p>
<p>Compound <strong>6</strong> has been previously identified in the CEE of the <em>Siparuna</em> species investigated in the present study [<a href="#B31-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-ijms-26-00633">31</a>]. Compound <strong>3</strong> was isolated from an alkaloidal extract of <em>S. decipiens</em> [<a href="#B44-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B44-ijms-26-00633">44</a>]. Compounds <strong>1</strong>, <strong>3</strong>, <strong>4</strong>, and <strong>7</strong>, in turn, have already been identified in the BuOH extracts of <em>S. cymosa</em>, while compounds <strong>1</strong> and <strong>2</strong> have been identified in the same extract of <em>S. glycycarpa</em> [<a href="#B31-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-ijms-26-00633">31</a>]. Compound <strong>5</strong> has been isolated from <em>Siparuna tonduziana</em> Perk [<a href="#B43-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B43-ijms-26-00633">43</a>].</p>
<p>Natural products serve as rich sources of active compounds with diverse chemical and biochemical structures. The evaluation of antiviral activities of certain medicinal herbal extracts against CoV suggests that the phytochemicals contributing to the inhibitory effects may include flavonoids, terpenoids, and other phenolic compounds, along with alkaloids, which are prominent components in the extracts that demonstrated inhibition of viral replication and enzymatic activity [<a href="#B1-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B1-ijms-26-00633">1</a>,<a href="#B19-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B19-ijms-26-00633">19</a>,<a href="#B21-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B21-ijms-26-00633">21</a>].</p>
<p>Several studies have reported that flavonoids and alkaloids are the major compounds in <em>Siparuna</em> species [<a href="#B28-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B28-ijms-26-00633">28</a>,<a href="#B31-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-ijms-26-00633">31</a>,<a href="#B46-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B46-ijms-26-00633">46</a>]. A review of phytochemical studies of 18 species of this genus reported the isolation of 74 substances. Of these, 28 are alkaloids, 16 of which are derived from the aporphine nucleus, and liriodenine is the most cited as an isolated alkaloid among the species [<a href="#B30-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B30-ijms-26-00633">30</a>].</p>
<p>Alkaloids have stood out for showing significant antiviral activity and/or have been used as prototypes in the development of synthetic antiviral drugs, with several investigations of their actions against different types of coronaviruses, including SARS-CoV-2 [<a href="#B47-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-ijms-26-00633">47</a>,<a href="#B48-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B48-ijms-26-00633">48</a>,<a href="#B49-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B49-ijms-26-00633">49</a>]. The isoquinoline alkaloids are the second-largest class of alkaloids after indole alkaloids and may be divided into 13 classes: bis-benzylisoquinolines, aporphines, benzyltetrahydro-isoquinoline, benzylisoquinolines, benzophenanthridine, cularines, morphinans, naphythylisoquinoline, pavines, phthalideisoquinoline, promorphinans, protoberberine, and ipecac alkaloids [<a href="#B39-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-ijms-26-00633">39</a>].</p>
<p>In view of their great structural diversity, isoquinoline alkaloid derivatives obtained from different plant sources have been reported to show antiviral effects on various targets. Studies have shown that compound <strong>6</strong> isolated from <em>Actinodaphne hookeri</em> Meissn, could inhibit viral replication of the Herpes Simplex virus [<a href="#B50-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B50-ijms-26-00633">50</a>]; compound <strong>3</strong> collected from <em>Peumus boldus</em> Molina, has been investigated for the anti-influenza A virus [<a href="#B51-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B51-ijms-26-00633">51</a>]; and compound <strong>4</strong> isolated from the family Lauraceae, showed a significant inhibitory effect on HIV-1 integrase [<a href="#B52-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B52-ijms-26-00633">52</a>].</p>
<p>Kumar et al. [<a href="#B53-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-ijms-26-00633">53</a>] used various computational tools to identify the most eligible drug candidate with the capabilities to halt the replication of the SARS-CoV-2 virus by inhibiting M<sup>pro</sup> (3CL<sup>pro</sup>). From a library of 100 molecules, it was observed that among the top 10 molecules, eight were alkaloid derivatives, with two aporphine alkaloids (boldine <strong>3</strong> and laurolitsine <strong>4</strong>) and laurolitsine having the highest binding affinities. This signifies that the structure of the alkaloid derivatives has the desired pharmacophoric features to perfectly bind and halt the activity of the enzyme.</p>
<p>The compound <strong>7</strong> was active against the RNA virus <em>Parainfluenza</em> (PI-3) and was employed for antiviral assessment of the compounds using Mad-ine-Darby bovine kidney and Vero cell lines [<a href="#B54-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B54-ijms-26-00633">54</a>]. An in silico study showed that compounds <strong>1</strong> and <strong>2</strong> inhibited colorectal cancer pathogenic gene products better than their native ligands, with the best binding energy [<a href="#B55-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B55-ijms-26-00633">55</a>].</p>
<p>Compound <strong>1</strong> obtained from <em>Cocculus hirsutus</em> (L.) W. Theob. was found to be promising with significant binding affinity to SARS-CoV-2 3CL<sup>pro</sup> in comparison to the control and showed stable interactions with the amino acid residues present on the active site of the protease, being considered a promising anti-COVID-19 candidate [<a href="#B56-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B56-ijms-26-00633">56</a>].</p>
<p>Alkaloids have also been reported to have good binding affinities with important enzymes of SARS-CoV-2 in bioinformatics studies [<a href="#B47-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B47-ijms-26-00633">47</a>,<a href="#B57-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B57-ijms-26-00633">57</a>,<a href="#B58-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B58-ijms-26-00633">58</a>]. Norboldine, an aporphine alkaloid that can be extracted from the roots of <em>Lindera aggregate</em> (Sims) Kosterm., has binding potential with three target proteins: ACE2, M<sup>pro</sup> (3CL<sup>pro</sup>), and PL<sup>pro</sup> [<a href="#B59-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-ijms-26-00633">59</a>]. Magnoflorine is another important aporphine-type alkaloid obtained from <em>Tinospora cordifolia</em> (Willd.) Hook. f. and Thomson, and displayed a good binding energy to 3CL<sup>pro</sup> and S protein of SARS-CoV-2 and ACE2 [<a href="#B14-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B14-ijms-26-00633">14</a>]. Moreover, Ellinger et al. [<a href="#B59-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B59-ijms-26-00633">59</a>] demonstrated the potential of emetine and papaverine to reduce the cytotoxic effects of SARS-CoV-2 in certain tissues.</p>
<p>Brown et al. [<a href="#B12-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B12-ijms-26-00633">12</a>], using a protein-based FRET-biosensor to identify inhibitors of 3CL<sup>pro</sup> from SARS-CoV-2, identified 19 compounds that have EC<sub>50</sub> values below 1 µM, five (N-allylnorapomorphine, R(−)-propylnorapomorphine, R(−)-2-hydroxyapomorphine, and R(−)-2,10,11-trihydroxy-N-propylnoraporphine) of which were aporphine alkaloids not previously identified as 3CL<sup>pro</sup> inhibitors, suggesting a promising starting point for structure-activity-relationship studies to develop new antiviral compounds.</p>
<p>In exploring the potential structure-activity relationships of isoquinoline alkaloids, Sharma et al. [<a href="#B39-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B39-ijms-26-00633">39</a>] underscored the significance of a nitrogen atom, particularly quaternary, and the presence of methyl groups within the structure of bioactive molecules. Compounds with such attributes, such as berberine, offer indirect support in combating diseases, enhancing immune responses (immunomodulation), or modulating pro-inflammatory reactions [<a href="#B60-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B60-ijms-26-00633">60</a>]. Moreover, other methoxylated alkaloids, including tetrandrine, neferine, berbamine, cephranthine, and hernandezine, have demonstrated substantial potential for combating SARS-CoV-2 infection by impeding viral entry into host cells. These compounds disrupt various pathways involved in the entry stage, including the inhibition of clathrin-mediated fusion induced by Ca<sup>2+</sup> influx, as well as the blockade of two-pore channel 2 and TRPMLs [<a href="#B61-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B61-ijms-26-00633">61</a>,<a href="#B62-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B62-ijms-26-00633">62</a>,<a href="#B63-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B63-ijms-26-00633">63</a>,<a href="#B64-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B64-ijms-26-00633">64</a>]. Furthermore, these compounds may target other crucial phases of viral infection, such as replication. Ghareeb et al. [<a href="#B65-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B65-ijms-26-00633">65</a>] and Varghese et al. [<a href="#B66-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B66-ijms-26-00633">66</a>] demonstrated that berberine inhibits 3CL<sup>pro</sup> and PL<sup>pro</sup> activity while also suppressing the expression of both the E-gene and RNA-dependent RNA polymerase (RdRp). These findings collectively highlight the multifaceted potential of isoquinoline alkaloids in combating viral infections and underscore their importance in therapeutic development.</p></section><section id="sec2dot4-ijms-26-00633"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.4. Siparunaceae Extracts Inhibit SARS-CoV-2 Replication in Human Infected Lung Cell Model</h3>
<p>After confirming that the extracts exhibited inhibitory activity against viral proteases and the interaction between the viral spike protein and cellular ACE2, we assessed their ability to inhibit viral replication in a complex cellular system. For this purpose, experimental assays were performed using the human pneumocyte line Calu-3, acknowledged as an optimal model for in vitro infection and the screening of anti-coronavirus agents due to its elevated susceptibility to SARS-CoV-2 via a transmembrane serine protease 2 (TMPRSS2)-dependent entry mechanism [<a href="#B53-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B53-ijms-26-00633">53</a>,<a href="#B67-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B67-ijms-26-00633">67</a>,<a href="#B68-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B68-ijms-26-00633">68</a>,<a href="#B69-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B69-ijms-26-00633">69</a>]. Initially, Calu-3 cells were subjected to infection at a multiplicity of infection (MOI) of 0.01. The extracts were then tested for their antiviral efficacy at a concentration of 50 μg·mL<sup>−1</sup> over a 24-h treatment period to identify the plant species demonstrating the most effective antiviral properties (<a href="#ijms-26-00633-f008" class="usa-link">Figure 8</a>). The extracts’ impact on viral replication in Calu-3 cells revealed that 20 (80%) extracts resulted in a reduction of viral activity by over 50%, and 10 (40%) of them inhibited more than 80% of viral replication under the same previously described conditions.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-26-00633-f008"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 8.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11765762_ijms-26-00633-g008.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e0e0/11765762/f3c80464688b/ijms-26-00633-g008.jpg" loading="lazy" height="434" width="704" alt="Figure 8"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-26-00633-f008/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Calu-3 cells infected with SARS-CoV-2 at an MOI of 0.01, were treated with 25 extracts from <em>Siparuna</em> species at 50 μg·mL<sup>−1</sup> for 24 h at 37 °C, 5% CO<sub>2</sub>. C: crude ethanolic extract; H: hexane; D: dichloromethane; E: ethyl acetate; B: butanol. Viral titers were assessed by plaque assay in PFU/mL and compared to infected and untreated controls (<em>n</em> = 4). Dotted lines indicate 70% and 90% inhibition.</p></figcaption></figure><p>To evaluate the toxicity of compounds in the Calu-3 cell model, we exposed the cells to the maximum possible concentration of the extract, ensuring that the dimethyl sulfoxide (DMSO) content did not exceed 1% in the culture medium to prevent interference with cellular viability from DMSO (<a href="#ijms-26-00633-f009" class="usa-link">Figure 9</a>). It was observed that the components of the extracts were non-toxic to this cellular model, as 23 (92%) extracts facilitated at least 70% cellular viability, with 18 (72%) achieving viability above 80%.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-26-00633-f009"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 9.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11765762_ijms-26-00633-g009.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e0e0/11765762/bd93baf30514/ijms-26-00633-g009.jpg" loading="lazy" height="491" width="791" alt="Figure 9"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-26-00633-f009/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Calu-3 cells were treated with the 25 extracts of <em>Siparuna</em> species at 200 μg·mL<sup>−1</sup> for 72 h at 37 °C and 5% CO<sub>2</sub>. C: crude ethanolic extract; H: hexane; D: dichloromethane; E: ethyl acetate; B: butanol. Cell viability was assessed by analyzing the activity of the enzyme lactate dehydrogenase (LDH) and comparing it with that of the untreated controls. (<em>n</em> = 3). Dotted lines indicate 70% and 90% of inhibition.</p></figcaption></figure><p>These results indicate that the concentration required for the extracts to cause any cellular damage is at least four times higher than the concentration needed to inhibit at least 50% of viral replication. This significant difference between the therapeutic efficacy and cytotoxicity underscores the potential safety and effectiveness of Siparunaceae extracts as anti-SARS-CoV-2 agents. The ability to achieve substantial antiviral effects at low concentrations, where cellular damage is minimal, highlights their promise in the bioprospecting of new anti-SARS-CoV-2 compounds. These results not only reinforce previous observations regarding the potent antiviral capabilities of Siparunaceae extracts, as discussed by our research group and others [<a href="#B31-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B31-ijms-26-00633">31</a>,<a href="#B33-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B33-ijms-26-00633">33</a>,<a href="#B70-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B70-ijms-26-00633">70</a>], but also highlight the specific impact of these botanical compounds on a relevant model of respiratory viral infection. The high efficacy of certain extracts in blocking SARS-CoV-2 replication suggests that bioactive compounds within these plants, possibly alkaloids and flavonoids, interact with viral particles or cellular mechanisms in a manner that markedly disrupts the viral life cycle.</p>
<p>This potent antiviral effect, particularly in a respiratory cell model, supports the potential therapeutic application of Siparunaceae extracts in treating infections caused by SARS-CoV-2. Further pharmacological exploration is required to identify the active constituents and to understand their mechanisms of action. Such research could pave the way for the development of novel antiviral agents derived from Siparunaceae, offering a natural and effective option for managing pandemics similar to COVID-19.</p></section><section id="sec2dot5-ijms-26-00633"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.5. Results from ADMET Predictions</h3>
<p>Initially, absorption, distribution, metabolism, excretion, and toxicity (ADMET) predictions were conducted using the Swiss ADME and Osiris DataWarrior platforms (<a href="#app1-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials Table S1</a>). The aim of this analysis was to identify compounds with favorable pharmacokinetic, pharmacodynamic, and pharmacological profiles, indicating their potential for further development. All analyzed compounds have a high probability of being absorbed through the gastrointestinal tract and crossing the blood-brain barrier (BBB). Additionally, none of them showed a significant probability of toxicity to the reproductive system, nor were they likely to be skin irritants, mutagenic, or tumorigenic. The only exception was compound <strong>1</strong>, which showed a high probability of tumorigenic activity.</p>
<p>The potential interactions of the seven identified compounds with the five major isoforms of cytochrome P450 (CYP1A2, CYP2C19, CYP2C9, CYP2D6, and CYP3A4) were investigated (<a href="#ijms-26-00633-t002" class="usa-link">Table 2</a>). Interestingly, only the compound bulbocapnine was predicted to have the potential to inhibit all CYP isoforms, especially the CYP2C19 and CYP2C9 isoforms, for which no other compound was predicted as an inhibitor.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/ijms-26-00633-t002/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>The gastrointestinal absorption and brain penetration of the seven identified compounds were illustrated using the Boiled Egg diagram, as depicted in <a href="#ijms-26-00633-f010" class="usa-link">Figure 10</a>, using the Swiss ADME web tool. In the figure, the white region represents the area with the highest likelihood of absorption by the human gastrointestinal system, while the yellow portion (the yolk) indicates the region with the highest likelihood of brain penetration. The diagram revealed that all examined compounds fell within the yellow region, suggesting a high probability of absorption by the human gastrointestinal tract and brain permeation.</p>
<figure class="fig xbox font-sm" id="ijms-26-00633-f010"><h4 class="obj_head">Figure 10.</h4>
<p class="img-box line-height-none margin-x-neg-2 tablet:margin-x-0 text-center"><a class="tileshop" target="_blank" href="https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/core/lw/2.0/html/tileshop_pmc/tileshop_pmc_inline.html?title=Click%20on%20image%20to%20zoom&amp;p=PMC3&amp;id=11765762_ijms-26-00633-g010.jpg"><img class="graphic zoom-in" src="https://cdn.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/blobs/e0e0/11765762/0fa6c682983c/ijms-26-00633-g010.jpg" loading="lazy" height="472" width="733" alt="Figure 10"></a></p>
<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="figure/ijms-26-00633-f010/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div>
<figcaption><p>Boiled egg diagram of the seven compounds under study. Points located in the yellow area indicate molecules predicted to passively permeate the blood−brain barrier (BBB). The white area represents the molecules passively absorbed by the human gastrointestinal tract (HIA). Blue points indicate molecules predicted to be effluxed from the central nervous system by P-glycoprotein.</p></figcaption></figure></section><section id="sec2dot6-ijms-26-00633"><h3 class="pmc_sec_title">2.6. Docking Analysis</h3>
<p>The seven compounds under study were subjected to molecular docking simulations to analyze the probability of interaction with the proteases of SARS-CoV-2, with the results demonstrated in three different scoring functions corresponding to the MolDock Score, Rerank Score, and PLANTS Score (the binding energy values of the compounds under study for the proteins related to the inhibition of the SARS-CoV-2 virus can be viewed in <a href="#app1-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials Tables S2–S7</a>).</p>
<p>Prior to conducting the docking, redocking was performed to validate the methodology under study, given that redocking corresponds to the Root Mean Square Deviation (RMSD) value of the ligand. It is considered ideal for the RMSD value in docking to be equal to or less than 2.0 Å. Thus, an RMSD less than 2.0 Å is widely accepted as a discriminant in reproducing a known binding mode, indicating the success of the method [<a href="#B71-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B71-ijms-26-00633">71</a>]. The ligands were selected based on their similarity to the compounds under study according to the criteria of heavy atoms, chemical groups, and molecular mass. The co-crystallized ligands available in the crystallography made available in the PDB are representative and biologically relevant: since they were experimentally validated, it has been demonstrated in reference articles [<a href="#B72-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B72-ijms-26-00633">72</a>,<a href="#B73-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73-ijms-26-00633">73</a>,<a href="#B74-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B74-ijms-26-00633">74</a>,<a href="#B75-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B75-ijms-26-00633">75</a>,<a href="#B76-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B76-ijms-26-00633">76</a>,<a href="#B77-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B77-ijms-26-00633">77</a>] that they represent a relevant biological interaction with the target studied. In this way, it is possible to show that the docking protocol is capable of predicting a pose similar to the experimental one. According to the obtained RMSD value, it can be observed that it was below 2.0 Å (<a href="#ijms-26-00633-t003" class="usa-link">Table 3</a>). These data indicated that the generated pose correctly positioned the ligand in the active site, suggesting that the program provided values that were considered satisfactory for docking validation.</p>

<div class="p text-right font-secondary"><a href="table/ijms-26-00633-t003/" class="usa-link" target="_blank" rel="noopener noreferrer">Open in a new tab</a></div></section><p>Regarding the 3D structures (PDB 3SCI and 6M0J) of the Spike protein [<a href="#B72-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B72-ijms-26-00633">72</a>,<a href="#B73-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B73-ijms-26-00633">73</a>], it was observed that they do not have co-crystallized ligands. The active site of these proteins was identified through the reference article of the PDB and the use of molecular pocket predictions such as the Bite Net Platform—Skoltech I Molecule, 2022 [<a href="#B78-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B78-ijms-26-00633">78</a>]. The coordinates used for the identification of the active site of the Spike protein were X: 12.70 and −32.26, Y: −6.44 and 21.10, and Z: 60.95 and −8.16 for PDBs 3SCI and 6M0J, respectively. For the Spike protein, remdesivir was used as a positive control [<a href="#B79-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link" aria-describedby="B79-ijms-26-00633">79</a>].</p>
<p>The docking results regarding the total probability values for the enzymes under study can be viewed in the main text and in the <a href="#app1-ijms-26-00633" class="usa-link">Supplementary Materials (Supplementary Materials Tables S2–S7)</a>. From this perspective, it can be observed that the compounds under study exhibited negative binding energy values in all enzymes studied, indicating interactions with the targets under study. The following sections discuss the results related to the compounds’ affinity to the targets under study.</p>
<section id="sec12"><h4 class="pmc_sec_title">Docking in Different Targets of SARS-CoV-2</h4>
<p>The results of the probability of activity for the target Spike protein, 3CL<sup>pro</sup>, and PL<sup>pro</sup> are shown in <a href="#ijms-26-00633-t004" class="usa-link">Table 4</a>. All test compounds displayed <em>p</em> enzyme values above 0.6. In the Spike protein, the PDB ligand (positive control) exhibited the highest affinity (<em>p</em> enzyme 0.98). Notably, bulbocapnine (<strong>7</strong>) had the second-highest activity probability for the Spike protein in PDB 3SCI (<em>p</em> = 0.77), while reticuline (<strong>2</strong>) ranked second for PDB 6M0J (<em>p</em> = 0.78). These compounds maintained high affinity for the <em>p</em> enzyme, with compound <strong>7</strong> at <em>p</em> = 0.73 and compound <strong>2</strong> at <em>p</em> = 0.77. For 3CL<sup>pro</sup>, compound <strong>2</strong> had the highest <em>p</em> enzyme value (<em>p</em> = 0.90), followed by boldine (<strong>3</strong>) (<em>p</em> = 0.89). The <em>p</em> enzyme value of the PDB was lower (<em>p</em> = 0.72) than that of these compounds. Compound <strong>7</strong> also had the highest total probability value for the 3D structure with code 6M2N (<em>p</em> = 0.94), and for PDB 7B3E, compound <strong>3</strong> had the highest activity probability value (<em>p</em> = 0.89). In turn, for PL<sup>pro</sup>, the PDB ligand showed the highest affinity, with the <em>p</em> enzyme equal to 0.99. Actinodaphine (<strong>6</strong>) stood out with a probability value of <em>p</em> = 0.62 for PDB 7LBR, while for PDB 7TZJ, the highest value (<em>p</em> = 0.84) was from compound <strong>7</strong>. Considering both targets, compounds <strong>6</strong> (<em>p</em> Enzyme 0.69) and <strong>7</strong> (<em>p</em> Enzyme 0.66) had the highest probability values after the ligand.</p>
